Good. Now I understand the predicates. Let me now follow the workflow step by step.

The predicate `dlistrep_shape(l, p)` means a doubly-linked list starting at `l` with prev pointer `p`. If `l == 0`, it's empty. Otherwise, it unfolds into `data_at(l->next, t) * data_at(l->prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(t, l)`.

The predicate `dllseg_shape(x, xp, yp, y)` means a segment from `x` to `y` (y not included), where x->prev = xp and yp->next = y.

Now for the function:
```
struct list *iter_back(struct list *l, struct list *head)
/*@ With l_prev
      Require dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)
    Ensure  dlistrep_shape(__return, 0)
 */
```

The precondition says: there's a segment from head to l (head's prev = 0, l's prev = l_prev), connected to a dlistrep from l with prev = l_prev. Together they form a full doubly-linked list from head (with prev = 0) all the way around.

The postcondition says: return a dlistrep starting at __return with prev = 0.

Let me think about the function again:
1. If l == 0, return head. The precondition when l == 0 is `dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, 0) * dlistrep_shape(0, l_prev)`. Since dlistrep_shape(0, l_prev) = emp, this is just `dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, 0)`. For the segment to end at 0, we need the segment to be the full list from head to 0, so `dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, 0) = dlistrep_shape(head, 0)` (when the segment ends at 0 with the right params). Actually wait, `dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, 0)` means the segment starts at head, head's prev is 0, and the last node in the segment is l_prev whose next points to 0. So this is essentially the full list from head to 0, meaning dlistrep_shape(head, 0). So returning head is correct.

2. If l != 0: p = l, then loop while (p != head) { p = p->prev; }. Starting from l, walk backward via prev until reaching head. Then return head (since p == head when loop exits).

- p starts at l and walks backward

Let me look at the problem from yet another angle.

The function: walks p from l backward to head using p = p->prev.

I think the cleanest invariant that matches what QCP can handle is:

```
dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, pprev)
```

Where `pprev` is a ghost variable representing the node right before p (in the prev sense, i.e., the node that was reached by p->prev = pprev... wait no, "prev" in the struct sense means the backward link. So p->prev points to a node earlier in the list. When p moves, p = p->prev, so the new p is what used to be pprev.

Hmm wait, if we think about the `prev` field: p = p->prev moves p backward in the list. The node "before" p (closer to head) is p->prev. The node "after" p (further from head) is the one that has p as its prev, which we can call p->next or something (but that's the forward direction).

For dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p):
- Starts at head (head->prev = 0)
- Ends at p (boundary, p is NOT in the segment... wait, param 4 is the endpoint marker not included)
- pprev is the last node IN the segment (param 3)
- pprev->next = p

This means: head -> ... -> pprev (segment, inclusive of pprev). Then pprev->next = p.

And dlistrep_shape(p, pprev) means list starting at p with prev = pprev, going until 0.

Together: dlistrep_shape(head, 0) = dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, pprev)

This is a proper split of the list at position p.

Let me check against RP_pre_loop (p = l):
dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev) ✓
→ pprev = l_prev, p = l

For RP_iter_2 (p = l_prev):
Invariant: dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, l_prev) * dlistrep_shape(l_prev, pprev)

But actual: dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * data_at(&(l->prev), l_prev) * data_at(&(l->next), y_290) * dlistrep_shape(y_290, l)

The invariant says: segment ends at l_prev (i.e., head -> ... -> prev_of_l_prev -> l_prev (segment boundary, not in seg))
but actual state has segment going further to l: head -> ... -> l_prev -> l (segment boundary, l not included)

So the invariant with p = l_prev would give a shorter segment than what's actually in the state, meaning more nodes are "exposed" in the actual state (from the segment side) than the invariant says.

The invariant dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, pprev) actually captures the state where p is the current node, and the list is split at p.

Actually, the way QCP works with loop invariants is:
1. Verify that the state before the loop implies the invariant (entry)
2. Verify that the invariant + loop condition + loop body + !loop condition re-establishes the invariant (preservation)
3. Verify that the invariant + !loop condition implies the postcondition (exit)

For entry: the state before the loop is the precondition after p = l. Since p = l and the precondition matches the invariant, this should work.

For preservation: after executing the body once, the state should imply the invariant again.

For exit: invariant + p == head should imply dlistrep_shape(head, 0).

For the exit: if the invariant is dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev), and p == head, we need dlistrep_shape(head, 0).

dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev) = dlistrep_shape(head, 0) in general (assuming l_prev is indeed l's predecessor in the list, which it is by the dllseg definition).

So the exit condition is satisfied: the invariant implies the postcondition. ✓

For preservation: after p = p->prev where p = l, the new p = l->prev. The state becomes:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * data_at(l->prev, l_prev) * data_at(l->next, y_290) * dlistrep_shape(y_290, l)

Need to show this implies: dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

But these are SEPARATED by *. So showing the second from the first requires:
  data_at(l->prev, l_prev) * data_at(l->next, y_290) * dlistrep_shape(y_290, l) ⊢ dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

Which is true by the definition of dlistrep_shape (folding). So if QCP's strategies include a folding rule for dlistrep_shape, this would work.

The unfolding doesn't directly expose yp's prev! It only exposes each node's prev as the previous node. The last node (yp) has its next pointing to y (the endpoint), but its prev is the second-to-last node in the segment.

This is exactly the folding direction of the predicate definition! If the strategy has a folding rule for dlistrep_shape, this works.

So I think the issue is whether the strategies include FOLDING rules in addition to UNFOLDING rules. Many QCP strategies only have unfolding (since unfolding is what's needed for the "forward" direction of execution). But entailments often require folding (going from an unfolded state back to a folded predicate).

Actually, I realize there might be a subtle issue. The invariant `dlistrep_shape(head, 0)` describes all the heap memory (the list). But the loop body also needs to access `p->prev`. For this, QCP needs `p` to be some node in the list. But `p` is not necessarily `head`. After several iterations, p could be deep in the list.

When QCP tries to verify `p = p->prev`, it needs to read `p->prev`. The current state has `dlistrep_shape(head, 0)`. QCP would need to unfold this predicate from `head` until it finds `p`. This could require many unfoldings.

But QCP's unrolling depth is 3, which means strategies might only unfold 3 levels deep. If p is deeper than 3 nodes from head, the strategy can't reach it.

However, the symbolic execution in QCP tracks the values of p at each program point. So after unfolding `dlistrep_shape(head, 0)` at head, the strategy might know that `p` is not head (since p != head from the loop guard) and needs to go deeper.

```

The left side has `(l_pre <> 0)` && `(l |-> l_pre)` ** dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) ** dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev).

The right side wants: EX p_prev, `(l_pre <> 0)` && `(l->prev |-> p_prev)` ** dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, l) ** dlistrep_shape(l, p_prev).

  (l->prev |-> l_prev) ** dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) ** dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

  dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) ** dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

The left has dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev) on the right, and the right has (l->prev |-> l_prev) ** dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev). We need dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev) ⊢ (l->prev |-> l_prev) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev).

This means unfolding dlistrep_shape one step: 
  dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev) = exists t. (l->next |-> t) * (l->prev |-> l_prev) * dlistrep_shape(t, l)

After unfolding: (l->prev |-> l_prev) * exists t. (l->next |-> t) * dlistrep_shape(t, l)
We need: ⊢ (l->prev |-> l_prev) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

So: exists t. (l->next |-> t) * dlistrep_shape(t, l) ⊢ dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

This is the FOLDING direction: unfolded nodes → folded predicate. The strategy needs a folding rule for dlistrep_shape.

Actually, the way QCP works with loop invariants is:

1. The invariant is checked BEFORE the loop body execution (at the loop head, before the condition check).
2. entail_wit_1 verifies the state at entry implies the invariant.
3. entail_wit_2 verifies that after executing the body, the state (which should now satisfy the invariant) does indeed imply the invariant.
4. return_wit_1 verifies that the invariant + !condition implies the postcondition.

For entail_wit_2, left side = state after body, right side = invariant.

Actually, the issue is not about the number of unfoldings. The strategies have specific patterns for when to unfold. If the pattern is "unfold if the first argument is non-null and not the endpoint", then unfolding happens at the head. Each time we unfold, we get the head node and the rest. This is about integer bounds, which should always be true. The strategy rule for this is probably a simple bound check.

```
Definition iter_back_return_wit_2 := 
forall (head_pre: Z) (l_pre: Z) (l_prev: Z) ,
  [| (l_pre = 0) |]
  && (dllseg_shape head_pre 0 l_prev l_pre)
  ** (dlistrep_shape l_pre l_prev)
|--
  (dlistrep_shape head_pre 0)
```

This is for the if-branch where l == 0: dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, 0) * dlistrep_shape(0, l_prev) ⊢ dlistrep_shape(head, 0).

dlistrep_shape(0, l_prev) = emp (first case of dlistrep: l == 0 && emp).
So this reduces to: dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, 0) ⊢ dlistrep_shape(head, 0).

A segment from head to 0 (endpoint) with last node = l_prev. Since the endpoint is 0, the segment must go all the way to 0. So dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, 0) = dlistrep_shape(head, 0) — the full list.

This needs a strategy rule that converts dllseg_shape ending at 0 to dlistrep_shape. The strategy might not have this rule.

5. return_wit_2: dllseg ending at 0 ⊢ dlistrep_shape (segment to list conversion)

Let me look at the goal again for entail_wit_2 with the p->prev invariant:

```
forall (head: Z) (p_prev_2: Z) (p: Z),
  [| (p <> head) |] && [| (p <> 0) |]
  && ((p->prev) |-> p_prev_2)
  ** (dllseg_shape head 0 p_prev_2 p)
  ** (dlistrep_shape p p_prev_2)
|--
  EX (p_prev: Z),
  [| (p_prev_2 <> 0) |]
  && ((p_prev_2->prev) |-> p_prev)
  ** (dllseg_shape head 0 p_prev p_prev_2)
  ** (dlistrep_shape p_prev_2 p_prev)
```

I realize now that the problem might be SIMPLER than I think. The left side is the state AFTER the loop body. The right side is the INVARIANT (what should hold). 

The left side has: `(p->prev |-> p_prev_2) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, p_prev_2)` — which is exactly the invariant form (dllseg_shape(head, 0, p->prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, p->prev)), PLUS the explicit `(p->prev |-> p_prev_2)`.

The right side wants: `(p_prev_2->prev |-> p_prev) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, p_prev_2) * dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev)` — which is the SHIFTED invariant (dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2->prev, p_prev_2) * dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev_2->prev)).

So QCP checks: can the state after body (which has the invariant at position p) imply the invariant at the SHIFTED position p_prev_2?

This shift happens because QCP checks the invariant at the LOOP HEAD (before condition). After the body executes:
- p has a new value
- The invariant must hold with the new p

So the invariant checked at the loop head uses the new p value. QCP evaluates:
  I(new p) = dllseg_shape(head, 0, new_p->prev, new_p) * dlistrep_shape(new_p, new_p->prev)

But new_p = old_p->prev = p_prev_2 (from the state). So:
  I(p_prev_2) = dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2->prev, p_prev_2) * dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev_2->prev)

And the state after body has:
  (old_p->prev |-> p_prev_2) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, old_p) * dlistrep_shape(old_p, p_prev_2)

These are different! I(p_prev_2) vs the state after body.

For the entailment to work:
  (old_p->prev |-> p_prev_2) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, old_p) * dlistrep_shape(old_p, p_prev_2)
  ⊢
  (p_prev_2->prev |-> p_prev) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, p_prev_2) * dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev)

From the state:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, old_p) — segment from head to old_p, last node = p_prev_2
  dlistrep_shape(old_p, p_prev_2) — list from old_p, prev = p_prev_2

To get the right side:
  Need p_prev_2->prev (inside the segment)
  Need dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2->prev, p_prev_2) — shorter segment ending at p_prev_2
  Need dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev_2->prev) — list from p_prev_2 with its prev

This requires peeling p_prev_2 from the segment and making it the start of the dlistrep. The segment dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, old_p) has p_prev_2 as its last node. We need to "pop" p_prev_2 from the segment and "push" it onto the dlistrep.

In predicate terms:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, old_p) * data_at(old_p->prev, p_prev_2) * data_at(old_p->next, t) * dlistrep_shape(t, old_p)
  
  We need to show this is equivalent to:
  exists p_prev. data_at(p_prev_2->prev, p_prev) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, p_prev_2) * data_at(p_prev_2->next, old_p) * data_at(p_prev_2->prev, p_prev) * dlistrep_shape(old_p, p_prev_2)
  
  Hmm, wait:
  dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev) unfolds to:
    data_at(p_prev_2->next, ...) * data_at(p_prev_2->prev, p_prev) * dlistrep_shape(..., p_prev_2)
  where ... = p_prev_2->next = old_p (since p_prev_2 is old_p's prev, so old_p is p_prev_2's next).
  
  So dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev) unfolds to:
    data_at(p_prev_2->next, old_p) * data_at(p_prev_2->prev, p_prev) * dlistrep_shape(old_p, p_prev_2)
  
  And the segment dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, p_prev_2) goes from head to p_prev_2, with last node = p_prev, endpoint = p_prev_2.
  
  So the right side expands to:
    data_at(p_prev_2->prev, p_prev) *
    data_at(p_prev_2->next, old_p) * data_at(p_prev_2->prev, p_prev) * dlistrep_shape(old_p, p_prev_2) *
    ... (segment unfolding) ...

And the left side is:
  data_at(old_p->prev, p_prev_2) * -- this matches p_prev_2->next = old_p, i.e., old_p has prev = p_prev_2, equivalently data_at(p_prev_2->next, old_p)
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, old_p) *
  dlistrep_shape(old_p, p_prev_2)

Hmm, for the left to match the right:
1. data_at(old_p->prev, p_prev_2) = data_at(p_prev_2->next, old_p) ← these are different fields!

old_p->prev = p_prev_2 means old_p's prev field is p_prev_2.
p_prev_2->next = old_p means p_prev_2's next field is old_p.
In a DLL, these are equivalent statements (if the list is well-formed). But they're DIFFERENT data_at predicates (different address).

So the left side has data_at(old_p->prev, p_prev_2), but the right side has data_at(p_prev_2->next, old_p). These are NOT the same data_at (different addresses), but they're logically equivalent in a DLL.

For the strategy to prove this, it would need a rule that connects `p_prev_2->next = old_p` with `old_p->prev = p_prev_2`. This is a DLL-specific rule.

I think this level of predicate manipulation might be beyond what the strategies can handle automatically.

Actually, let me try something different. What about using the initial invariant from the precondition but ALSO tracking p explicitly?

For example:
```
/*@ Inv Assert p != 0 && l != 0 &&
    dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) *
    dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)
 */
```

This is just the precondition. The invariant doesn't mention p's position relative to the list. But the loop body accesses p->prev.

With this invariant, QCP would handle p->prev like this:
1. First iteration (p = l): unfold dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev) to get l->prev. ✓
2. After p = l->prev = l_prev, p is inside the segment.
3. Second iteration (p = l_prev): need l_prev->prev. The state has dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l). Need to find l_prev->prev inside the segment.

For step 3, QCP would try to unfold the segment to find l_prev->prev. The segment dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) is structured as:
  head -> head->next -> ... -> l_prev -> (l)
where l_prev is the last node, l is the endpoint.

l_prev->prev is the node BEFORE l_prev in the chain. To find it, QCP needs to traverse the segment from head to l_prev.

The strategy might unfold the segment step by step:
- Unfold dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) → data_at(head->next, z1) * data_at(head->prev, 0) * dllseg_shape(z1, head, l_prev, l)
- Check if z1 = l_prev (no, l_prev is at the end)
- Keep unfolding: dllseg_shape(z1, head, l_prev, l) → ... → eventually reach l_prev

This requires unfolding the entire segment. For the strategies with depth-3, this works for segments up to length 3.

BUT WAIT — I just realized that the invariant I'm writing (`dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)`) has `l_prev` and `l` as parameters, not `p`. When QCP computes the invariant for the loop head, it uses this formula.

After the body executes (p changes from l to l_prev), QCP checks the invariant again. The formula doesn't mention p, so it's trivially the same. The entailment would be:

State after body ⊢ dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

If the state still has these predicates (possibly with some unfolded nodes), then QCP needs to fold them back. The dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev) was unfolded to access l->prev. So the state has:
  data_at(l->prev, l_prev) * data_at(l->next, t) * dlistrep_shape(t, l)

For the entailment: data_at(l->prev, l_prev) * data_at(l->next, t) * dlistrep_shape(t, l) ⊢ dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

This is a folding step — the reverse of the predicate definition. This might work if the strategy has a folding rule.

After the second iteration:
  p = l_prev. Read l_prev->prev. This requires unfolding the segment dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l).
  
  After unfolding, the state has: data_at(l_prev->prev, y) * data_at(l_prev->next, l) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, y, l_prev) * data_at(l->prev, l_prev) * data_at(l->next, t) * dlistrep_shape(t, l)
  
  For the invariant: dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) * dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)
  
  We need to fold: 
    data_at(l_prev->prev, y) * data_at(l_prev->next, l) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, y, l_prev) ⊢ dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l) 
  
  AND
    data_at(l->prev, l_prev) * data_at(l->next, t) * dlistrep_shape(t, l) ⊢ dlistrep_shape(l, l_prev)

The second is the same folding as before. The first requires extending the segment by one node (which is the definition of dllseg unfolding in REVERSE).

So this approach requires FOLDING for both dlistrep and dllseg. If the strategies have folding rules, this works. If not, it fails.

Hmm, I think I'm misunderstanding the segment structure. Let me re-read more carefully.

dllseg_shape(x, xp, yp, y):
- x = segment start (first node in the segment)
- xp = x->prev (prev of first node)
- yp = last node IN the segment
- y = endpoint marker (NOT in the segment, yp->next = y)

So the segment is: x → next → ... → next → yp, where yp->next = y (y is the first node after the segment).

If the segment starts at head and ends at l_prev (inclusive):
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, l_prev, l)
  
  This means: head -> z1 -> z2 -> ... -> l_prev, and l_prev->next = l.

    Since l_prev->next = l (by definition of the segment's endpoint), u = l.

  data_at(l_prev->next, l) * data_at(l_prev->prev, z_{k-1}) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, z_{k-1}, l_prev)

  No wait, the dllseg definition unfolds from the START, not the END. To FOLD from the end, we'd need to use the recursive definition in reverse:
  
  If we have dllseg_shape(x, xp, z, y) * data_at(z->next, y) * data_at(z->prev, x_k), does this fold to dllseg_shape(x, xp, y, y_next)?

  So folding at the END doesn't fit the definition structure. The segment grows from the START, not the end.

This is the fundamental problem. The segment can be extended by prepending a node (adding a new first node), not by appending one (adding a new last node). 

For our backward-traversal scenario, we need to extend the dlistrep at the START (prepend a node to the dlistrep). But dlistrep_shape also extends at the START:
  dlistrep_shape(p, pprev) = exists t. data_at(p->next, t) * data_at(p->prev, pprev) * dlistrep_shape(t, p)

  But this requires pprev_of_p_prev_2 which is the node before p_prev_2 in the list.

So the invariant extension works like this:
BEFORE: dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, p_prev_2)
AFTER:  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, p_prev_2) * dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev)

Where p_prev = p_prev_2->prev.

The dlistrep changes: from dlistrep_shape(p, p_prev_2) to dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev).

The dlistrep gets BIGGER by one node at the start (prepending p_prev_2).

For this shift, the strategy would need to:
1. Unfold dlistrep_shape(p, p_prev_2) to get p->prev (done for the assignment)
2. Combine the unfolded p's node with the segment dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev_2, p)
3. Extract p_prev_2's prev from the segment
4. Construct the new dlistrep_shape(p_prev_2, p_prev_2->prev)

The return_wit_1 checks: invariant + p = head ⊢ postcondition. The invariant at p = head is:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, head->prev, head) * dlistrep_shape(head, head->prev)

We need: dlistrep_shape(head, 0).

For dllseg_shape(head, 0, head->prev, head): the segment starts at head, head->prev = 0 (from second param), and the last node is head->prev = 0 (since head->prev = 0). So the segment is dllseg_shape(head, 0, 0, head).

By the base case: (head == head && 0 == 0 && emp) = emp.

So indeed, the invariant implies the postcondition. But the strategy needs to apply the base case, which requires knowing that the third parameter (0) equals the second parameter (0).

Actually, wait: the third parameter is p->prev = head->prev. In the symbolic state, head->prev is a FIELD ACCESS. QCP evaluates it in the context of the current state. The state has dllseg_shape(head, 0, ..., ...), which tells us head->prev = 0 (second parameter).

But does QCP know that p->prev = 0 when p = head? Let me think about this...

The invariant at the checkpoint is: dllseg_shape(head, 0, p->prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, p->prev).

QCP evaluates this formula in the current symbolic state. For p->prev, it looks at the symbolic value of p (which is now head) and the state. The state has dllseg_shape(head, 0, ..., ...) which says head->prev = 0. So p->prev = head->prev = 0.

But the strategy might not "evaluate" field accesses in this way. It might treat p->prev as a variable (let's call it P_prev_val) and try to prove the entailment with abstract values.

In the Coq goal, QCP replaces p->prev with a fresh variable. Looking at return_wit_1:
```
Definition iter_back_return_wit_1 := 
forall (p_prev: Z) (head: Z) (p: Z) ,
  [| (p = head) |] && [| (p <> 0) |] && [| (head <> 0) |]
  && ((&((p)  # "list" ->ₛ "prev")) # Ptr  |-> p_prev)
  ** (dllseg_shape head 0 p_prev p)
  ** (dlistrep_shape p p_prev)
|--
  (dlistrep_shape p 0)
```

The goal has p_prev as a universally quantified variable. The left side includes:
  (p->prev |-> p_prev)  — the data_at for p's prev, storing value p_prev
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, p)  — segment with last node = p_prev
  dlistrep_shape(p, p_prev) — list from p with prev = p_prev

And the constraint: p = head, p ≠ 0, head ≠ 0.

The right side: dlistrep_shape(p, 0).

So we need: from dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) * dlistrep_shape(head, p_prev), prove dlistrep_shape(head, 0).

dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head): by the base case, if p_prev = 0, the segment is emp. Then the state is dlistrep_shape(head, 0) = right side. ✓

But can we prove p_prev = 0? Not directly. p_prev is a universally quantified Z value. The only constraints are:
  p = head, p ≠ 0, head ≠ 0

We don't have a constraint that p_prev = 0. And from the state, dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) doesn't force p_prev = 0 (it's a parameter).

Wait, but the separation logic semantics: dllseg_shape(_, _, _, _) is defined as = (x==y && xp==yp && emp) || .... So dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) is either (head==head && 0==p_prev && emp) OR the recursive case. For the segment to be valid (i.e., for the formula to hold), either p_prev must equal 0 (base case) OR there must be a data_at(head->next, ...) in the state (recursive case).

But we have BOTH dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) and data_at(head->prev, p_prev) in the state. The dllseg says: head->next = ... and head->prev = 0. The data_at says: head->prev = p_prev. These must be consistent, so 0 = p_prev.

But does the strategy make this connection? The dllseg's base case requires xp == yp, i.e., 0 == p_prev. The data_at(head->prev, p_prev) with the dllseg's internal head->prev = 0 forces p_prev = 0.

Actually, I think the connection IS implicit in the separation logic: the data_at(head->prev, p_prev) is a SEPARATE predicate from dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head). The dllseg_shape predicate, when unfolded at head, would give:
  data_at(head->next, z) * data_at(head->prev, 0) * dllseg_shape(z, head, p_prev, head)

But the state also has data_at(head->prev, p_prev) separately. In separation logic, having two data_at predicates for the same location (head->prev) is a CONTRADICTION — they must have the same value. So p_prev must equal 0.

But the strategy might not resolve this contradiction automatically. It might try to unfold dllseg_shape and match the data_at from dllseg with the separate data_at.

For the strategy rule that handles this: "if the state has dllseg_shape(x, xp, yp, y) AND data_at(x->prev, xp) AND x == y, then dllseg = emp." This rule would work, but it requires the strategy to match p_prev with 0.

Hmm, maybe the problem is that p_prev appears in BOTH places (dllseg third param and data_at value), and the strategy doesn't have a rule that uses this information.

Let me think about this differently. In the return_wit_1 goal, the left side has:
  [p = head] ... dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) * dlistrep_shape(head, p_prev)

Since p = head, the dllseg is dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head). For this segment to be valid:
- Either head == head (true) AND 0 == p_prev — which gives us p_prev = 0
- Or the recursive case — which requires the existence of additional nodes

But the dllseg here is a PREDICATE, not a PROPOSITION. It's a piece of SEP that we hold. The predicate is defined as a formula that must hold. For dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) to be satisfied, we need either:
1. Base case: head == head && 0 == p_prev → so p_prev = 0
2. Recursive case: there's some z such that we have data_at for head's fields and a segment from z to head.

But if case 2, the segment would have nodes. Since head != head's next (a node can't point to itself in a valid list), and eventually the base case must apply (the segment terminates), the base case at the terminator requires the last node's prev to match. But that's deeper in the list.

Actually, for dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head), in the RECURSIVE case:
  exists z. data_at(head->next, z) * data_at(head->prev, 0) * dllseg_shape(z, head, p_prev, head)
  
  Now dllseg_shape(z, head, p_prev, head): start = z, z->prev = head, last = p_prev, endpoint = head.
  
  For this to terminate, eventually we need start == endpoint, i.e., some node = head. And at that point, xp == yp, meaning the start's prev (head's prev) = last (p_prev). But head's prev = 0 from the dllseg parameter, so p_prev = 0.
  
  So regardless of which case is used, the predicate semantics force p_prev = 0.

But this reasoning requires analysis of the predicate definition. The strategy might not do this analysis automatically. It might just try to match the syntactic structure.

The strategy needs a rule specifically for "dllseg_shape(x, xp, yp, x) → yp == xp" (i.e., when start == endpoint, the last node's prev must equal start's prev). This is a derived property of dllseg.

Without this rule, the strategy can't prove return_wit_1.

p_new = p_old->prev. So p_new is the node that is "before" p_old in the list. In the DLL, p_new->next = p_old (by the DLL property: if a->prev = b, then b->next = a). So p_new->next = p_old.

Actually, let me reconsider. Maybe `has_permission(&p)` DOES help in a different way. In QCP, `has_permission(&p)` gives ownership of the pointer p, which means the strategy knows it can dereference p. When executing `p = p->prev`, QCP resolves:

The unfolding strategy works like this:
- The state has dlistrep_shape(head, 0)
- The goal needs data_at(&(p_val->prev), ...)
- The strategy matches: p_val is in dlistrep_shape(_, _)
- It unfolds: dlistrep_shape(head, 0) = data_at(head->next, t) * data_at(head->prev, 0) * dlistrep_shape(t, head)
- Then checks: is p_val == head? If p_val = l (initial case), l != head, so no.
- Continues unfolding until p_val is reached.

This loop DEEPENS the predicates. With 3-depth unrolling, there might be a limit.

3. Sliding splits (for forward traversal)
4. Base cases (dllseg with start=endpoint, dlistrep with p=0)

For a BACKWARD traversal, the issue is that the re-splitting (step 3) requires shifing the split in the PREV direction, while the strategies might only handle NEXT direction.

Wait, but the invariant structure is the same regardless of traversal direction:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p->prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, p->prev)
  
The re-splitting after p = p->prev:
  From: dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, prev)
  To:   dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev', prev) * dlistrep_shape(prev, prev')
  
  where prev = p->prev and prev' = prev->prev.

This is a SHIFT of the split by one node toward head. In a FORWARD traversal (p = p->next):
  From: dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, prev)
  To:   dllseg_shape(head, 0, p, next) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  
  where next = p->next.

This is a SHIFT of the split by one node away from head.

The difference: in the forward case, the DLTREP shrinks (the first node of dlistrep moves to dllseg), while in the backward case, the DLLSEG shrinks (the last node of dllseg moves to dlistrep).

The sliding rules (13-16) might handle the FORWARD case (dlistrep → dllseg) but not the BACKWARD case (dllseg → dlistrep).

Let me check: for forward traversal:
  State before body: dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, prev)
  After p = p->next: unfold dlistrep_shape(p, prev) → (p->next |-> next) * (p->prev |-> prev) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  New state: dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) * (p->next |-> next) * (p->prev |-> prev) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  
  Need to show: dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) * (p->next |-> next) * dlistrep_shape(next, p) ⊢ dllseg_shape(head, 0, p, next) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  
  i.e., dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) * (p->next |-> next) * dlistrep_shape(next, p) ⊢ dllseg_shape(head, 0, p, next) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  
  This requires EXTENDING the segment at the END: dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) → dllseg_shape(head, 0, p, next).
  
  By the dllseg definition: dllseg_shape(head, 0, p, next) = dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) * data_at(p->next, next) * data_at(p->prev, prev) * dllseg_shape(next, p, p, next)?
  
  Actually, the segment extends at the END by making p the new last node:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p, next) = 
    (head == next && 0 == p && emp) || 
    exists z. data_at(head->next, z) * data_at(head->prev, 0) * dllseg_shape(z, head, p, next)
  
  This doesn't directly relate to dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p). The extension at the end requires changing the yp and y parameters from (prev, p) to (p, next).
  
  Hmm, this is complex. But the dllseg definition lets us "append" to the segment at the END by unfolding:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p, next) unfolds until we reach p:
    ... * data_at(p->next, next) * data_at(p->prev, something) * dllseg_shape(next, p, p, next) = emp
  
  And dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) unfolds until we reach prev:
    ... * data_at(prev->next, p) * data_at(prev->prev, something) * dllseg_shape(p, prev, prev, p)
    
    dllseg_shape(p, prev, prev, p): start = p, start_prev = prev, last = prev, endpoint = p.
    (p == p && prev == prev && emp) = emp ✓
  
  So dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) = [nodes from head to prev] * data_at(prev->next, p) * data_at(prev->prev, ...)
  
  And after the body: dllseg_shape(head, 0, prev, p) * (p->next |-> next) * (p->prev |-> prev) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  = [nodes from head to prev] * data_at(prev->next, p) * data_at(prev->prev, ...) * (p->next |-> next) * (p->prev |-> prev) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  
  = [nodes from head to prev] * dlistrep_shape(p, prev) * (p->next |-> next) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  
  Wait, dlistrep_shape(p, prev) = (p == 0 → emp) | (p->next |-> z) * (p->prev |-> prev) * dlistrep_shape(z, p)
  
  But we have (p->next |-> next) * (p->prev |-> prev) * dlistrep_shape(next, p) which is exactly the unfolded dlistrep_shape(p, prev) with z = next.
  
  So: dlistrep_shape(p, prev) = (p->next |-> next) * (p->prev |-> prev) * dlistrep_shape(next, p)
  
  And: [nodes from head to prev] * dlistrep_shape(p, prev) = dlistrep_shape(head, prev_prev_of_head) * ... hmm, this gets complicated.

Actually, the forward case is simpler because the strategy can unfold dlistrep_shape one step, then fold the segment.

For the backward case: the segment SHRINKS by one node at the END. The last node becomes the start of dlistrep. This requires either:
a) Unfolding the segment from the END (not directly supported)
b) Fully unfolding the segment and re-splitting
c) Using rules that specifically handle the backward case

Option (b) is what the strategy might do: unfold the entire segment until the last node is exposed, then re-split. This requires many unfoldings.

Option (c) implies that if the strategies have backward sliding rules, they handle this case.

Since the strategies couldn't prove entail_wit_2, it seems like neither option is available.

I think the FUNDAMENTAL issue is that the strategies are designed for forward traversal, and the backward traversal is not supported.

Actually, wait. Let me reconsider the problem. The function walks p backward through a doubly-linked list. Each step accesses p->prev. This is a prev-field read.

Actually, I think I need to understand how QCP resolves field reads. For `p->prev`:
- p has a symbolic value (let's say `p_val`)
- QCP needs to find a predicate `data_at(&(p_val->prev), ..., result_val)` in the SEP
- If it can find it, the result is `result_val`

The strategy rules include unfolding rules for dlistrep/dllseg. When the strategy encounters `data_at(&(p_val->prev), ..., result_val)`, it checks if any predicate in the SEP has `p_val` as a parameter that matches the unfolding pattern.

For dlistrep_shape(head, 0): the first argument is head, not p_val. So the rule that unfolds dlistrep_shape for `p_val` would NOT fire (since p_val is NOT the first argument).

This is the root cause! The strategy rule checks if the pointer in the goal matches the first argument of dlistrep_shape. Since p is not the first argument (head is), the rule doesn't apply.

Hmm wait, the entailment is for the state BEFORE the body, not after the unfolding. In QCP's verification:
1. The invariant holds at the checkpoint
2. The loop body (p = p->prev) is executed
3. After the body, the state must imply the invariant

So the unfolding happens during symbolic execution of the body, and the entailment is checked on the RESULTING state.

Let me trace through QCP's handling of `p = p->prev`:

State before body: invariant I(p) = dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, pprev)
where pprev = p->prev.

Body: p = p->prev
  - Read p->prev: need to find value. Strategy unfolds dlistrep_shape(p, pprev):
    dlistrep_shape(p, pprev) → (p->next |-> t) * (p->prev |-> pprev) * dlistrep_shape(t, p)
  - Extract pprev = p->prev_val (the value of p->prev = pprev from the invariant?)
    Actually, the value pprev is already known from the invariant (it's the third param of dllseg). So p->prev = pprev.
  - Assign p = pprev

After body: p_new = pprev, state = dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p_old) * (p_old->next |-> t) * (p_old->prev |-> pprev) * dlistrep_shape(t, p_old)

Where p_old is the old p value, p_new is the new p value (which equals pprev).

Now check invariant I(p_new):
  I(p_new) = dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_new->prev, p_new) * dlistrep_shape(p_new, p_new->prev)
  
  p_new = pprev. p_new->prev = pprev->prev = ??? (unknown from the state)
  
  So: I(p_new) = dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev->prev, pprev) * dlistrep_shape(pprev, pprev->prev)

The state after body has:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p_old) * (p_old->next |-> t) * (p_old->prev |-> pprev) * dlistrep_shape(t, p_old)

The entailment is: state after body ⊢ I(p_new)

The key challenge: from dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p_old), find pprev->prev.

dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p_old) has pprev as the LAST node in the segment. pprev->prev is buried inside the segment.

The strategy would need to repeatedly unfold dllseg_shape from the front until reaching pprev, then extract pprev->prev.

With depth-3 unrolling of the loop, the segment could be any length. But the PREDICATE can be unfolded any number of times (unfolding is not limited by loop unrolling depth).

The question is whether the STRATEGY has rules to iteratively unfold a dllseg to reach a specific node. Looking at the strategies:
- Rules 1-4: might handle dllseg unfolding at the front
  - Rule 1: x → generic
  - Rule 2: x → generic
  - Rule 3: x, p → might be dllseg unfolding: dllseg_shape(x, xp, yp, y) ⊢ ...
  - Rule 4: x, p → might be dllseg elimination

If the strategy can unfold dllseg_shape from the front, it can go:
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p_old) → data_at(head->next, z1) * data_at(head->prev, 0) * dllseg_shape(z1, head, pprev, p_old)

And then each subsequent step unfolds the new segment. But the question is: does this STRATEGY know to keep unfolding until pprev is reached?

The strategy works by PATTERN MATCHING. The goal is to prove I(p_new). The strategy tries to match the goal structure against the state. For the segment:
  Goal needs: dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev->prev, pprev)
  State has: dllseg_shape(head, 0, pprev, p_old)

These are DIFFERENT terms. The strategy doesn't "unfold until pprev is found" — it tries to directly MATCH the goal term against the state.

If the goal is I(p_new) which mentions pprev->prev, the strategy needs to find pprev->prev in the state. It doesn't unfold the segment iteratively — it tries to match the pattern.

So the strategy needs a RULE that matches: state with dllseg_shape(_, _, pprev, p_old) and goal with dllseg_shape(_, _, pprev->prev, pprev). This is a SLIDING rule that transforms the split.

Rules 13-16 might be these sliding rules. They have many parameters (matching both the old and new split positions).

But the backward case: old split = (pprev, p_old), new split = (pprev->prev, pprev). This is moving pprev OUT of the segment into the dlistrep position.

For the FORWARD case: old split = (pprev, p), new split = (p, p->next). This is moving p OUT of the dlistrep into the segment.

The sliding rules might only handle the FORWARD case (dlistrep → dllseg), not the BACKWARD case (dllseg → dlistrep).

This would explain why entail_wit_2 is Admitted.

Actually, in separation logic, `has_permission` (or `any` in some logics) is used when you have permission but don't know the exact value. For reading, you'd get a fresh symbolic value.

1. Permission: has_permission(&p) grants it ✓

forall (p_prev: Z) (head: Z) (p: Z),
  [| (p = head) |] && [| (p <> 0) |] && [| (head <> 0) |]
  && ((&((p)  # "list" ->ₛ "prev")) # Ptr  |-> p_prev)
  ** (dllseg_shape head 0 p_prev p )
  ** (dlistrep_shape p p_prev )
|--
  (dlistrep_shape p 0 )
```

This goal doesn't include any PROP from the invariant (except p != 0 and head != 0). It only uses the conditions from the invariant check. Wait, it also has p = head from the loop condition being false.

So the left side has:
- p = head, p ≠ 0, head ≠ 0
- (p->prev |-> p_prev)  — data_at from the invariant's p->prev evaluation
- dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) — since p = head
- dlistrep_shape(head, p_prev)

The right side: dlistrep_shape(head, 0)

For p_prev = 0: dllseg_shape(head, 0, 0, head) = (head == head && 0 == 0 && emp) = emp. Then the left is dlistrep_shape(head, 0). ✓

For p_prev ≠ 0: dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) would need to be satisfied by the recursive case. But the recursive case requires data_at(head->next, ...), and the state only has data_at(head->prev, p_prev). So the dllseg can't be satisfied unless p_prev = 0.

But the STRATEGY doesn't do this analysis. It tries to match the goal syntactically.

The solution for return_wit_1: prove that p_prev = 0 from the state. But p_prev is a universal variable, and the only constraint is from the data_at(dllseg).

Actually, looking at this from the Coq perspective: the SEP on the left has:
  (p->prev |-> p_prev)  — this data_at and the dllseg's internal data_at must be consistent
  dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head)  — this contains data_at(head->prev, 0) when unfolded

But the SEP has data_at(p->prev, p_prev) and also (implicitly) data_at(head->prev, 0). Since p = head, these are data_at for the SAME field (head->prev). So p_prev must equal 0.

Yes! The SEP has:
1. (head->prev |-> p_prev) from (p->prev |-> p_prev) with p = head
2. dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) which contains data_at(head->prev, 0) when unfolded

But these are CONFLICTING! The same field head->prev is claimed to have TWO different values. In separation logic, this would mean the entailment is invalid UNLESS p_prev = 0.

For the entailment to be valid, the strategy would need to combine the data_at from dllseg (head->prev = 0) with the explicit data_at (head->prev = p_prev) and deduce p_prev = 0.

But wait, in QCP, dllseg_shape is a folded predicate. The data_at(head->prev, 0) is INSIDE the predicate, not visible to the entailment checker. The entailment checker sees:
  data_at(head->prev, p_prev) * dllseg_shape(head, 0, p_prev, head) * dlistrep_shape(head, p_prev)
  ⊢
  dlistrep_shape(head, 0)

For this, the strategy needs to unfold dllseg_shape once:
  data_at(head->prev, p_prev) * 
  [data_at(head->next, z) * data_at(head->prev, 0)] *
  dllseg_shape(z, head, p_prev, head) *
  dlistrep_shape(head, p_prev)
  ⊢
  dlistrep_shape(head, 0)

Now we have TWO data_at for head->prev: p_prev and 0. For separation logic consistency, p_prev = 0. If the strategy recognizes this, it can substitute.

Then: data_at(head->next, z) * data_at(head->prev, 0) * dllseg_shape(z, head, 0, head) * dlistrep_shape(head, 0) ⊢ dlistrep_shape(head, 0)

Since dllseg_shape(z, head, 0, head) would need to be satisfied, we'd need to unfold further... eventually dllseg would become emp and we'd have dlistrep_shape(head, 0). But this requires many steps of analysis.

I don't think the strategy engine handles this level of reasoning automatically. The issue is that the entailment requires UNFOLDING the segment and MERGING the explicit data_at with the segment's internal data_at, which requires the strategy to recognize that they refer to the same field.

OK, I'm convinced now that the standard strategies can't handle this case. Let me try one final creative approach and then conclude.

What if I add the PROP constraint `p->prev == 0` to the invariant? This would give the strategy the needed equality. But it's only true at exit (p = head). At other times (p ≠ head), p->prev ≠ 0. So this property is not invariant.

UNLESS... I don't put it in the invariant but instead derive it from the postcondition? No, the invariant is checked at the loop head, and the postcondition is checked at function exit.

What if I rearrange the loop to use a conditional that adds the PROP at the right time? I can't modify the loop structure.

What if I use a DIFFERENT invariant that doesn't have this issue? Like one where at p = head, the segment is automatically empty without needing equality reasoning?

For that, I'd need dllseg_shape(head, 0, 0, head) = emp. The base case requires 0 = 0 (head's prev = 0). But the invariant would need to track that head's prev is 0.

The invariant `dllseg_shape(head, 0, p->prev, p) * dlistrep_shape(p, p->prev)` has dllseg's SECOND parameter as 0, which encodes head->prev = 0. At p = head: dllseg_shape(head, 0, head->prev, head). For the base case: head == head && 0 == head->prev → need 0 = head->prev. This is given by the SECOND parameter (0) and the THIRD parameter (head->prev).

But the third parameter is generalized: it's p->prev, not necessarily 0. When p = head, p->prev = head->prev. And head->prev = 0 from the second parameter. So p->prev = 0.

The strategy needs to connect: second param (0) = third param (p->prev) when p = head. This connection is that both describe head->prev. But the strategy operates syntactically and doesn't "know" that the third param of dllseg has anything to do with head->prev.

UNLESS the strategy has a RULE specifically for dllseg_shape(x, 0, p, x) that derives p = 0. Looking at rules 5-6:
- Rule 5: y, q, prev, p
- Rule 6: y, prev, p

Rule 5 with parameters (y, q, prev, p) could match dllseg_shape(..., ..., ..., y) and extract y->prev. Rule 6 with parameters (y, prev, p) could match dllseg_shape(x, xp, y, y) and extract y->prev = p when x=y and xp=p.

These ARE the rules I need! Rule 6 specifically matches when start = endpoint and extracts the prev. And Rule 5 might handle the backward traversal.

But the goals are STILL Admitted, meaning these rules didn't fire or didn't close the goals. Why?

The rules might need specific conditions to fire:
- Strategy rules have preconditions (like variable types, non-null constraints)
- If a precondition isn't met, the rule doesn't fire

For example, rule 6 might need y to be non-null. But we know p = head != 0 (from PROP), so y = head should be non-null.

Or rule 6 might need the fourth arg (y) to equal the first arg (x). This is only true at exit (p = head). In the general case, the rule would try to match but fail because x ≠ y. The strategy might then backtrack and try other rules.

For return_wit_1, the condition p = head IS given. So x = head, y = head. The rule should fire.

Hmm, maybe the issue is not rule 6 but something else about the entailment. Let me run the p->prev invariant one more time and see the symexec log to find what rules were applied.
